Information Flow Control in Cyclic Process Networks
Bas van den Heuvel, Farzaneh Derakhshan, Stephanie Balzer

Abstract
Protection of confidential data is an important security consideration of today’s applications. Of particular concern is to guard against unintentional leakage to a (malicious) observer, who may interact with the program and draw inference from made observations. Information flow control (IFC) type systems address this concern by statically ruling out such leakage. This paper contributes an IFC type system for message-passing concurrent programs, the computational model of choice for many of today’s applications such as cloud computing and IoT applications. Such applications typically either implicitly or explicitly codify protocols according to which message exchange must happen, and to statically ensure protocol safety, behavioral type systems such as session types can be used. This paper marries IFC with session typing and contributes over prior work in the following regards: (1) support of realistic cyclic process networks as opposed to the restriction to tree-shaped networks, (2) more permissive, yet entirely secure, IFC control, exploiting cyclic process networks, and (3) considering deadlocks as another form of side channel, and asserting deadlock-sensitive noninterference (DSNI) for well-typed programs. To prove DSNI, the paper develops a novel logical relation that accounts for cyclic process networks. The logical relation is rooted in linear logic, but drops the tree-topology restriction imposed by prior work.

1
Introduction

Many of today’s emerging applications and systems
such as cloud computing and IoT applications
are inherently
concurrent
and
message passing
.
Message passing also enjoys popularity in mainstream languages such as Erlang, Go, and Rust.
Similar to functional languages with the
\lambda
-calculus as their theoretical model,
the model of message-passing concurrent languages is the process calculus
[
29
,
45
,
46
]
.
A program in this setting amounts to a number of
processes
connected by
channels
,
which compute by exchanging messages along these channels,
rather than by
\beta
-reductions or writing to and reading from shared memory.
Messages may even include channels themselves,
a feature supported in the
\pi
-calculus
[
47
,
57
]

and referred to as
higher-order
message passing.

Originally untyped
[
47
]
, the
\pi
-calculus has gradually been enriched with types
to prescribe the kinds of messages that can be exchanged over a channel
[
57
]

and to assert correctness properties, such as deadlock freedom and data-race freedom
[
38
,
36
,
37
,
40
]
.
Following in these footsteps,

session types

[
30
,
31
]
were conceived
to additionally express the
protocols
underlying the exchange.
Session types rely on a
linear
treatment of channels to model the state transitions induced by a protocol,
which was even substantiated by a Curry-Howard correspondence
between the session-typed
\pi
-calculus and
linear logic
[
9
,
65
,
66
,
62
,
43
,
10
,
44
]
.
Session types based on linear logic enjoy strong properties,
comprising not only race and deadlock freedom but also protocol fidelity.

Security is another correctness consideration arising from today’s applications and systems.
One security concern in particular is the protection of confidential information,
by preventing unintentional leakage to a (malicious) observer,
who may interact with the program and draw inference from made observations.
Type systems for
information flow control (IFC)
rule out such leakage by type
checking
[
64
,
58
,
55
]
,
given a lattice over security levels and the labeling of observables (e.g., output, locations, channels) with these levels.
Well-typed programs then prevent ”flows from high to low” and
guarantee
noninterference
, i.e., that an observer cannot infer any secrets from made observations.
To guarantee noninterference, advanced proof methods
such as logical relations
[
52
,
2
,
22
,
48
,
35
,
61
]

and bisimulations
[
42
,
60
,
59
,
56
]
are used.
If side channels
[
55
]
, such as the termination channel, are present,
then the literature distinguishes
progress-sensitive
noninterference (PSNI)
from
progress-insensitive
noninterference (PINI),
where the former only equates a divergent program run with another diverging one,
whereas the latter equates a divergent program run with any other run
[
24
]
.

Whereas the development of IFC type systems has been an active research field for decades
for imperative and predominantly sequential languages,
their exploration in a concurrent, message-passing setting has been more confined to typed process
calculi
[
32
,
33
,
17
,
25
,
39
,
67
,
53
]

and multiparty session types
[
13
,
11
,
14
,
12
]
.
Only recently, IFC has been adopted for session types based on linear logic
[
20
,
5
]
.
The resulting type systems exploit the strong guarantees arising from linear logic,
which in particular curtail the network of processes arising at runtime to a tree structure.
However, many real-world application scenarios
are precluded from an insistence on a tree structure, instead requiring support of
cyclic process networks
.
Session type systems
[
6
,
7
,
19
,
27
,
28
]

that allow for cyclic process networks increase expressivity while remaining rooted in linear logic.

This paper scales IFC to cyclic process networks
and contributes an IFC type system for an asynchronous
\pi
-calculus with linear session types.
To prove that well-typed processes in the resulting language enjoy noninterference,
we develop a novel logical relation.
Our development was challenged by the possibility of
deadlocks
that can arise in cyclic process networks
and that constitute another form of side channel.
To rule out side-channel attacks due to deadlocks,
we introduce the notion of
deadlock-sensitive noninterference (DSNI)
,
which only equates a deadlocking program with another deadlocking one.
Using our logical relation we prove that well-typed processes in our language enjoy DSNI (fundamental theorem).

Cyclic process networks also turn out to be beneficial for IFC,
as they permit secure programs that are rejected by existing IFC type systems for linear session types
[
20
,
5
]
.
These are programs that exploit the possibility of setting up several channels—rather than just one channel—between two processes,
to separate low-security from high-security communication.

Contributions.

Our contributions are threefold:

1.

An IFC session type system for an asynchronous
\pi
-calculus with support for cyclic process networks (
Sec.

3
), that satisfies protocol fidelity and communication safety (
Thm.

12
).

2.

A logical relation that induces an equivalence between typed processes (
Sec.

4
), defining our notion of DSNI (
Def.

21
).

3.

The main result that well typedness implies DSNI (
Thm.

23
in
Sec.

5
), following from the fundamental theorem (
Thm.

29
).

Outline.

In addition to the above contributions,
Sec.

2
gently introduces the key ideas behind the developments in this paper,
Sec.

6
discusses related work, and
Sec.

7
concludes the paper.
Important proofs are detailed in part; remaining details, proofs, and auxiliary definitions are given in the .

2
Key Ideas

In this section, we discuss the key ideas behind our contributions.

2.1
Cyclic Process Networks Afford Flexible Information Flow Control

We motivate our developments through a high-level example, focusing on how cyclicity in process networks improves over prior works by increasing the flexibility of information flow control to support more realistic scenarios.

Collaborating governments.

Consider two governments that want to collaborate on scientific and intelligence efforts.
Clearly, the interactions between a government and an intelligence agency is confidential, whereas interactions between a government and the scientific community is not; intelligence may not leak to the scientific community, where there may be spies.

We make this more precise by establishing that information can be of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh or
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow confidentiality, and that communication channels can be of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh or
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow security.
Clearly, information of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow confidentiality can be transmitted over
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-security channels, but not vice versa.
We identify our two governments as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{X}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{B}}\}
, each with departments (processes)
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{X}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{X}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{X}}}
.
We consider three scenarios, each of which connects these processes to form different process networks.

{subfigure}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{?}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}

Figure 1:
Scenario 1: No cyclicity.

{subfigure}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}

Scenario 2: Doubly connected governments.

{subfigure}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{A}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{B}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{B}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{B}}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}

Figure 3:
Scenario 3: Extended cyclicity for more flexible IFC.

Figure 4:
Collaborating governments: three scenarios.

Scenario 1: No cyclicity.

In
Fig.

4
, the governments have only one channel to communicate on.
Lines between departments denote communication channels, and their annotations indicate security levels.
In this scenario, no processes are cyclically connected.

Notice that the channel connecting the two governments does not have a security level assigned.
If we insist on intelligence exchange (i.e., on exchanging
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-confidentiality information), this channel must be of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh security.
However, this inhibits scientific exchange: when a government receives information on a
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-security channel, it cannot guarantee that the information is of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow confidentiality, so it cannot share it with its scientific department over a
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow-security channel.
Hence, the single channel of communication between the governments is unrealistic.

Scenario 2: Doubly-connected governments.

In
Fig.

4
, we attempt to remedy this problem by adding a second channel of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow security between the governments.
Since the governments are connected on two separate channels, they are cyclically connected.

Now the governments can exchange scientific information and share this with their intelligence agencies.
However, it is conceivable that a government makes decisions about which scientific information to share based on intelligence information.
A clever spy may then be able to infer intelligence information from scientific information,
indirectly
.
Hence, once a government receives intelligence information, it should refrain from sharing scientific information.
Clearly, this scenario is still not realistic.

Scenario 3: Extended cyclicity for more flexible IFC.

In
Fig.

4
, we split our governments into
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh- and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow-confidentiality departments.
The
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-confidentiality departments share intelligence information, and the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow-confidentiality departments share scientific information.
Crucially, the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow-confidentiality department can share information with the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-confidentiality department, but not vice versa.

2.2
Threats to Noninterference due to Deadlocks

When process networks contain cyclic connections, there is a risk of
deadlocked
communication.
For example, consider again the process network in
Fig.

4
.
Let us refer to the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-security channel between the governments as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{h}}
, and the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow-security channel between the governments as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{l}}
.
Suppose the two governments are implemented as follows (in pseudocode):

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{receive\ on\ h;send\ on\ l}}\hfill{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{B}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{receive\ on\ l;send\ on\ h}}\hfill

The communication between the governments is deadlocked: each government is waiting to receive from the other, but the corresponding sends are blocked.

In process networks without cyclicity, this kind of deadlock does not occur.
There are ways to prevent them through typing (cf., e.g.,
[
19
,
28
,
7
]
),
but the possible occurrence of deadlock introduced by cyclicity is realistic and a possible threat to noninterference.
To see how, consider another pseudocode implementation, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{s_{A}}}
refers to the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow-security channel between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{A}}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{receive\ x\ on\ h;if\ x==true\ then\ send\ on\ s_{A}\ else\ deadlock}}

(1)

In this scenario, a spy monitoring the information exchanged on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{s_{A}}}
is indirectly able to infer
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-confidentiality information: if information is sent on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{s_{A}}}
, then the spy knows for sure that the value of x is true.
This is why we are after IFC for noninterference that is
deadlock sensitive
.

2.3
IFC Type System in a Nutshell

In this paper, we implement IFC similarly as in previous works: by enriching a session type system with IFC annotations and requirements.
We build on the session-typed asynchronous variant of the
\pi
-calculus of Van den Heuvel and Pérez
[
27
,
28
]
(stripped from the “priority” mechanisms that rule out deadlock).

As anticipated in
Sec.

2.1
, channels are appointed
maximum-secrecy levels
(secrecy levels, for short), indicating the maximum secrecy of messages that can be sent on channels securely. For example, in
Fig.

4
, the channel between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
has a secrecy level of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh, while the channel between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{A}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
has a secrecy level of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow. This indicates that a spy with low-level security clearance can observe the messages sent on channels of secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow but not
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh.
A partial order on these secrecy levels forms a
secrecy lattice
; for example,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L\sqsubseteq H}}
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow is lower than
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh).

As processes receive messages on channels, they learn “secrets”, possibly influencing the information sent in future messages (referred to in the literature as
flow sensitivity

[
55
]
).
Key in our IFC is thus that it is forbidden to send messages on a channel if the level of secrecy learned so far exceeds the secrecy level of the channel.
To ensure this, our type system assigns to each process a
running secrecy
that increases as the process receives higher-secrecy-level information.
As processes evolve, the secrecy levels of their channels do not change, whereas their running secrecies do.

For example, in
Fig.

4
, suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
starts with a
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow running secrecy, thus being able to send messages to both
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{A}}}
.
After receiving a message from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
, the running secrecy of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh: the secrecy level of the channel between them is
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh.
Hence, after this message,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
can no longer send messages to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Science_{A}}}
.

Finally, we need to address how our IFC handles deadlock sensitivity, as introduced in
Sec.

2.2
.
It turns out that it is sufficient to rely on running secrecies and their dynamics as described above.
To see how, consider again the implementation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
in (
1
).
Assuming it starts with
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow running secrecy, the process receives on a
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh-security channel, so its running secrecy becomes
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}
igh.
Our IFC then disallows the process from sending on the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
ow security channel.
Hence, this example would be considered ill typed in our type system.

2.4
Logical Relation for DSNI in a Nutshell

Let us be more precise in what we mean by DSNI.
A process may have a number of “unconnected” channels.
By connecting these channels to other processes, we create a
context
in which to run the process.
We refer to the channels connecting the process to its context as the
interface
. For example, in
Fig.

4
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
can be considered as a standalone process, with the rest of the processes being a potential context for it. The interface is then the four channels connecting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
to the other processes.

With DSNI, we assume the existence of an “attacker”, a more precise definition of the “spy” mentioned in
Sec.

2.1
.
This attacker knows the specification of our process, and has the ability to observe messages from and to the process over
observable channels
: channels in the interface that have secrecy levels up to a given secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}
.
Moreover, the attacker cannot measure time but can observe the relative order in which messages are sent through different channels.
By running our process in different contexts and observing the messages on observable channels, the attacker may be able to use its knowledge of the process’ specification to infer information about messages on unobservable channels.
As such, noninterference means that the attacker is not able to do so; in our case, we are after DSNI, because we do not want the attacker to infer information from deadlocks either.

In this paper, we define DSNI as an
equivalence
between the behavior on observable channels of the same process in different contexts.
This equivalence is defined by means of a
logical relation
.
The relation scrutinizes messages from and to the process on observable channels, and “ignores” messages on unobservable channels.
Our main result is that well typedness implies DSNI (
Thm.

23
).

2.5
Technical Challenges

The subsequent sections first introduce our process language and then develop an IFC type system for that language and state and prove DSNI using a logical relation. These sections are naturally quite technical. To bridge the divide, we briefly survey here the main challenges our development had to overcome.

Asynchronous communication

Our process language is an
asynchronous

\pi
-calculus with linear session types, based on Van den Heuvel and Pérez’s Asynchronous Priority-based Classical Processes (APCP)
[
27
,
28
]
, but without recursion and “priority” mechanisms (which prevents deadlocks).
As in the asynchronous
\pi
-calculus, outputs in our calculus do not have any continuations but are atomic processes composed in parallel with other processes.
To model session sequencing, a process must adopt a
continuation-passing
style, in the sense that an output not only comprises a message but also a continuation channel.

When continuation channels are part of messages exchanged over an observable channel in the interface between a process and its context,
the question comes up whether the continuation channel becomes observable as well.
The natural impulse might be to consider them observable too. For sure this is the right choice in linear session-typed process calculi that confine process networks to trees
[
20
,
5
]
, guaranteeing that the continuation channel sent as part of the message resides within the sending process itself.
However, due to the possibility of cycles in our setting, a continuation channel sent as part of a message may actually reside within the context outside the sending process. As a result, the logical relation has to consider the binding structure of the process and the context when determining observability of continuation channels.
We detail this case analysis in
Sec.

4
when we introduce the logical relation, with a pictorial illustration in
Fig.

13
.

Observable deadlocks

Deadlock-sensitive noninterference (DSNI) provides a very strong notion of noninterference in that it equates a deadlocking process only with another deadlocking one (as opposed to an arbitrary one). As a result, it prevents leakage through deadlocks, a side channel similar to the termination channel.
DSNI is asserted by the definition of the logical relation and challenges the proof of
Thm.

23
, stating that well typedness implies DSNI.

Thm.

23
is proved a generalized
fundamental theorem
(
Thm.

29
), which asserts that all executions of a process, if well typed, are related by the logical relation, up to the secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}
of the observer.
Because this theorem relates two different processes, but with the same observable behavior (where deadlocks are observable), the proof must maintain a tight correspondence between the two processes.
This correspondence is achieved by employing the notion of
relevant nodes
(
Def.

27
), which are the parts of a process that can have observable outcomes either directly (by sending a message over the interface) or indirectly (by initiating a chain of messages ending with an observable one), and asserting that the relevant nodes of both processes are indistinguishable (up to structural congruence).
Our notion of relevant nodes is inspired by Derakhshan et al.
[
20
]
, but accounts for cyclic process networks.

Structural congruence and alpha equivalence

Our logical relation makes use of structural congruence to single out the action in a process producing an observable message.
Because structural congruence permits alpha renaming, process relatedness must account for alpha-equivalence classes.
As usual, proofs require a careful treatment of alpha renaming, which additionally becomes more nuanced by the existence of binders for observable names in contexts.
This treatment becomes especially apparent in the so-called
catch-up
lemma (
Lem.

32
), a lemma used in the proof of the fundamental theorem to assert that two observably equivalent processes can “catch up” on each other’s unobservable reductions.

3
Linear Session Types for Information Flow Control

In this section, we define our information flow control (IFC) type system.
We first introduce our process language (an asynchronous
\pi
-calculus) along with a linear session-type system in
Sec.

3.1
.
Then, we enrich the type system with IFC in
Sec.

3.2
.
In
Sec.

3.3
, we prove that well-typed processes enjoy communication safety and protocol fidelity as corollaries of a type-preservation result.
As we will see in
Sec.

5
, well typedness in the resulting IFC type system implies noninterference.

3.1
Process Language: Syntax, Semantics, and Types

Our process language is an asynchronous
\pi
-calculus, where parallel subprocesses communicate on connected channels.
To be precise, we adapt the non-recursive fragment of Van den Heuvel and Pérez’s Asynchronous Priority-based Classical Processes (APCP)
[
27
,
28
]
by removing their “priority” mechanisms that prevent deadlock and adding our IFC.

Syntax.

The syntactic elements of our language are typeset in a
black and non-italic
font.
In our language, channels have two distinct endpoints, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{c}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}
and further referred to as
names
.
By design, all names are used linearly, meaning that they are used for a communication exactly once.

Definition 1

(Syntax)
.

Processes

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{R}},\ldots
are defined by the following syntax:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{R}},\ldots::={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{0}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(P\mathbin{|}Q)}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)P}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[]}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x();P}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(z)\mathbin{\triangleright}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[a,b]}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(y,z);P}}

We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\{x/y\}}}
to denote the capture-avoiding substitution of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
.
Process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{0}}
denotes inaction.
In
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(P\mathbin{|}Q)}}
, processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
run in parallel; we often omit the parentheses.
Restriction
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)P}}
binds
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
to form a channel, enabling communication.

Process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[]}}
closes the channel to which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
belongs, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x();P}}
waits for the channel to close before continuing as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
.
Selection
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j}}
sends the label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{j}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
along with a name
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
; we refer to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
as the selection’s
continuation
, as it provides a means to continue communicating after the selection.
Branch
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(z)\mathbin{\triangleright}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}
waits to receive on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{j}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{I}}
along with a continuation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
before continuing as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{j}\{b/z\}}}
; this binds
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}
in each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{i}}}
.
Send
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[a,b]}}
sends names
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
; we typically refer to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
as the send’s payload and continuation, respectively, but there is no technical distinction between them.
Receive
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(y,z);P}}
waits to receive on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
two names
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
before continuing as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\{a/y,b/z\}}}
; this binds
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
.
All names in a process are free unless bound as described above; we write
\fn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
to denote the set of free names of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
.

Example 2
.

To illustrate process syntax, we further develop the example introduced in
Sec.

2.1
.
We develop two simple accounts of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}}}
: one where information flow is secure, and one where it is not.

In the first scenario,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}
passes a research outcome (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{oc}}
) to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
, which determines a command for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1^{\prime}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1})(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}[a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}oc_{2}\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1}[])}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1})\mathbin{\triangleright}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{oc_{1}:(\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}act\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[]),}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{oc_{2}:(\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}wait\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[])}}\end{array}\right\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{i_{A}(i_{A}^{1})\mathbin{\triangleright}\{act:i_{A}^{1}();0,wait:i_{A}^{1}();0\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A_{secure}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}})(\nu a_{I}i_{A})(Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}\mathbin{|}Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}\mathbin{|}Int_{A})}}

In the second scenario,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
receives intelligence (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{int}}
) from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}
and shares the information (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{inf}}
) with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Int_{A}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu i_{A}^{1^{\prime}}i_{A}^{1})(i_{A}[i_{A}^{1}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}int_{1}\mathbin{|}i_{A}^{1}[])}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}(a_{I}^{1})\mathbin{\triangleright}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{int_{1}:(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1})(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}[a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}inf_{1}\mathbin{|}a_{I}^{1}();a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}[]),}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{int_{2}:(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1})(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}[a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}inf_{2}\mathbin{|}a_{I}^{1}();a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}[])}}\end{array}\right\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1})\mathbin{\triangleright}\{inf_{1}:a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1}();0,inf_{2}:a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1}();0\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A_{insecure}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}})(\nu a_{I}i_{A})(Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}\mathbin{|}Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}\mathbin{|}Int_{A})}}

Variants of the
\pi
-calculus often include the forwarder process
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{[x\mathbin{\leftrightarrow}y]}}
which forwards any communications between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
by fusing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
.
Here, we choose to omit forwarders for a smoother definition of our logical relation; they can be added as syntactic sugar using
identity expansion
(cf. the on
Def.

34
).

Semantics.

The dynamics of our language is defined in terms of a reduction semantics, where each step represents the synchronization of complementary communications on the two endpoints of a channel.
As usual, reduction relies on structural congruence, which restructures processes without affecting channel connections and the order of communications.

Structural congruence (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
):

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{sc-alpha}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv_{\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to28.2516pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 2.78421pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{sc-par-nil}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 13.48878pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to28.97757pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\mathbin{|}0}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{sc-par-symm}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 19.65541pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to41.31082pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\mathbin{|}Q}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q\mathbin{|}P}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{sc-par-assoc}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 36.22751pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to74.45502pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\mathbin{|}(Q\mathbin{|}R)}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{sc-res-symm}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 31.41226pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to64.82452pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu yx)P}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{sc-res-assoc}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 53.96071pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to109.92142pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(\nu zw)P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu zw)(\nu xy)P}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{sc-res-comm}]}}}}\\

Reduction (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
):

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-close-wait}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 37.64488pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to77.28978pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(x[]\mathbin{|}y();P)}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-sel-bra}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 74.59929pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{j}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{I}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to163.53044pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(x[b]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}y(w)\mathbin{\triangleright}\{i:Q_{i}\}_{i\in I})}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{j}\{b/w\}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-send-recv}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 72.57944pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to147.15889pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(x[a,b]\mathbin{|}y(z,w);Q)}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q\{a/z,b/w\}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-sc}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.69054pt}\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.69054pt}\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to90.20284pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 33.98203pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-par}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.76382pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to43.67058pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\mathbin{|}Q}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}\mathbin{|}Q}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\mbox{\scriptsize{{{[{red-res}]}}}}\\
\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 21.52068pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to67.18428pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{\;\scriptsize}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)P^{\prime}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}

Figure 5:
Structural congruence (top) and reduction (bottom); cf.
Def.

3
.

Definition 3

(Reduction Semantics)
.

Structural congruence
is the least congruence on the syntax of processes (i.e., closed under arbitrary process contexts), denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
, induced by the axioms in
Fig.

5
(top).

Reduction
is a binary relation on processes, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
, defined by the rules in
Fig.

5
(bottom).
We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}}}
to denote that there is no
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
.

Rule [
sc-alpha
] allows alpha conversion, i.e., renaming bound names.
Rule [
sc-par-nil
] defines
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{0}}
as the unit of parallel composition, and Rules [
sc-par-symm
] and [
sc-par-assoc
] define parallel composition as symmetric and associative, respectively.
Rules [
sc-res-symm
] and [
sc-res-assoc
] define symmetry and associativity of restriction, respectively.
Rule [
sc-res-comm
] defines commutativity of restriction, as long as this does not capture or free any names; this is often referred to as
scope extrusion
.

Rules [
red-close-wait
], [
red-sel-bra
], and [
red-send-recv
] define synchronizations of complementary communications on names connected by restriction; these rules formalize the behavior described below
Def.

1
.
Rules [
red-sc
], [
red-par
], and [
red-res
] close reduction under structural congruence, parallel composition, and restriction, respectively.

Example 4
.

We illustrate process semantics on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A_{secure}}}
defined in
Example

2
.
We have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{A_{secure}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}})(\nu a_{I}i_{A})(Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}\mathbin{|}Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}\mathbin{|}Int_{A})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{I}i_{A})((\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1^{\prime}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1})((\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}})(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}[a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}oc_{2}\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1})\mathbin{\triangleright}\{\ldots\})\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1}[])\mathbin{|}Int_{A})}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{I}i_{A})((\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1^{\prime}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1})((\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}wait\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1^{\prime}}();a_{I}^{1}[])\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1}[])\mathbin{|}Int_{A})}},

from where asynchronous communication enables further communication between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1^{\prime}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}^{1}}}
or between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{i_{A}}}
; for example,

{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{I}i_{A})((\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}wait\mathbin{|}a_{I}^{1}[])\mathbin{|}Int_{A})}}.

Types.

We use linear session types to ‘‘tame’’ our processes.
The system we use is derived from classical linear logic, so types are expressed as linear-logic propositions
1
1

1

This choice is usually motivated as it comes with deadlock freedom, but we have two different reasons: (1) it allows for direct compatibility with session-type systems for deadlock freedom, and (2) a logical basis gives us a very clean and well-behaved linear session type system, which we can carefully extend to serve our goals (here, guaranteeing noninterference by typing).

; they are typeset in a
blue and sans-serif
font.

Definition 5

(Types)
.

Types

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{B}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{C}},\ldots
are defined by the following syntax:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{B}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{C}},\ldots::={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}\{i:A\}_{i\in I}}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\text{{\&}}\{i:A\}_{i\in I}}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\otimes}B}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}}B}}

Duality
is a unary operation on types, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A^{\bot}}}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1^{\bot}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}^{\bot}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\text{{\&}}\{i:A_{i}^{\bot}\}_{i\in I}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\otimes}B^{\bot}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A^{\bot}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}}B^{\bot}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot^{\bot}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\text{{\&}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}^{\bot}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}^{\bot}\}_{i\in I}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}}B^{\bot}}}

\displaystyle:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A^{\bot}\mathbin{\otimes}B^{\bot}}}

Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1}}
is associated with names that close channels, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot}}
with names that wait for channels to close.
Types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\text{{\&}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}
are associated with names that make and expect labeled selections, respectively; given
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{j}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{I}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A_{j}}}
is the type of the continuation after
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{j}}
has been selected/received.
Types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\otimes}B}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}}B}}
are associated with names that send and receive, respectively;
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{B}}
are the types of the payload and continuation afterwards.

Duality is a key component of session types, as it defines precisely what is meant by complementary behavior; for example,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1^{\bot}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot}}
is complementary to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1}}
.
Clearly, duality is an involution (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{(A^{\bot})^{\bot}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}}
).

Our type system is defined as a sequent calculus.
In the following, ignore the annotations in
red and italic
; these annotations are for IFC, explained in
Sec.

3.2
.

Figure 6:
Typing rules; cf.
Def.

6
.

Definition 6

(Type System)
.

Typing contexts

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Delta}},\ldots
are defined by the following syntax:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Delta}},\ldots::={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\emptyset}}\mathbin{\mbox{\large{$\mid$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:{A}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}

Typing judgments
are denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}}
.
They are derived using the rules in
Fig.

6
.

Typing contexts are thus sets of types assigned to names; the type system allows implicitly reordering these assignments in typing contexts.
Whenever we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma,\Delta}}
, we assume that the sets of names appearing in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Delta}}
are disjoint.

Rule [
typ-inact
] types inaction under empty context.
Rule [
typ-par
] types parallel composition by splitting the typing context into disjoint parts, one for each parallel process.
Rule [
typ-res
] types restriction by requiring the connected names to be dually typed.
Rule [
typ-close
] types a close with only its subject in the context.
Dually, Rule [
typ-wait
] types a wait by removing its subject from the context of the continuation.
Rule [
typ-sel
] types a selection; note that the continuation is typed dually to the continuation type of the selection itself, as this name will be received by a corresponding branch and used for further communications there.
Dually, Rule [
typ-bra
] types a branch; it requires every continuation to be typed with the same context besides the type of the continuation name.
Rule [
typ-send
] types a send; the payload and continuation are typed dually, similar to the continuation in Rule [
typ-sel
].
Dually, Rule [
typ-recv
] types a receive.

Example 7
.

To illustrate process typing, we type the secure variant of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
introduced in
Example

2
as follows.
We omit the
red and italic
IFC annotations entirely, as well as the typing of the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{oc_{2}}}
branch which is analogous to the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{oc_{1}}}
branch.

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.4291pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 58.59564pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to119.1913pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-sel}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}act}}\\
::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}:{\oplus}\{act:1,wait:1\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}}}:\bot\end{array}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 5.69054pt}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 5.7167pt\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 21.98618pt\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to45.97235pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-close}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1}[]}}::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1}}}:1}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to57.40576pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-wait}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[]}}\\
::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}}}:\bot,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1}}}:1\end{array}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to213.02524pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-par}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 24.90854pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}act\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[]}}\\
::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}}}:\bot,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}:{\oplus}\{act:1,wait:1\},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}}}:\bot,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1}}}:1\end{array}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 24.90854pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to163.20816pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-res}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 33.93344pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}act\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[])}}\\
::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}}}:\bot,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}:{\oplus}\{act:1,wait:1\}\end{array}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\hbox{\hskip 5.69054pt}\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{$\vdots$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to310.12563pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-bra}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1})\mathbin{\triangleright}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{oc_{1}:(\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}act\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[]),}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{oc_{2}:(\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}wait\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[])}}\end{array}\right\}}}::\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}:\&\{oc_{1}:\bot,oc_{2}:\bot\},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}:{\oplus}\{act:1,wait:1\}\end{array}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}\end{array}

3.2
Information Flow Control

We now enrich the type system presented thus far with IFC, such that well typedness guarantees noninterference (
Sec.

5
).
That is, we introduce and explain the annotations in
red and italic
in
Fig.

6
, and formalize the intuititions given in
Sec.

2.3
.

We use
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}},\ldots
to denote secrecy levels.
The relation between secrecy levels is denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c\sqsubseteq d}}
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c}}
is at most as secret as
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}}
), forming a lattice
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash\phi}}
to denote that the relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\phi}}
between secrecies holds within
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}
.
The least upper bound (join) and greatest lower bound (meet) are denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c\sqcup d}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c\sqcap d}}
, respectively.

Every name is assigned a secrecy level, denoted in typing contexts using square brackets after the name’s type, as in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:A{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}
.
To remember the level of secrecy of the messages received by a process, we annotate the process in typing judgments with a
running secrecy
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}
.
Given the running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}}
of the process before an input and the secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c}}
of the input’s subject, the input updates the running secrecy to the join
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqcup c}}
.
When a process then performs an output, to make sure that the name is secured for handling the secrecy level of the outgoing message, our IFC requires that its running secrecy is not higher than that of the output’s subject name.

We make these intuitions precise by discussing the IFC annotations on each typing rule in
Fig.

6
.
Since Rule [
typ-inact
] does not involve communication, no secrecy checks are necessary.
Rule [
typ-close
] types an output, so it requires that the running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}}
of the close is at most the secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c}}
of the closed name (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqsubseteq c}}
): the information received so far (the running secrecy) is not more secret than the closed name.
Rules [
typ-sel
] and [
typ-send
] also type outputs, so their checks are similar.
Rule [
typ-wait
] types an input, so it sets the running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}}}
of the continuation of the wait to the least upper bound of the running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}}
before the wait and the secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c}}
of the name of the wait (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}=d\sqcup c}}
; i.e., to the least secrecy level that is at least as high as both involved secrecies).
Rules [
typ-bra
] and [
typ-recv
] also type inputs, so they update running secrecies similarly.
Rule [
typ-par
] combines the running secrecies
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{2}}}
of the parallel processes by taking a secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}}
that is at most the greatest lower bound of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{2}}}
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
); this way, the parallel composition has a running secrecy of at most the least common secrecy level of its components, ensuring that each process in the composition has at least as much information as the parallel composition itself.
Rule [
typ-res
] requires that the secrecies of the connected names coincide, ensuring that secrecy checks are consistent on both names of the created channel.
Note that channels in typing contexts may have different secrecy levels.
However, typing rules enforce that the channels in the same session (e.g., a send and its continuation) have the same secrecy levels.
For example, Rule [
typ-sel
] ensures that channel
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
and its continuation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
are both of the same secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{c}}
.

Example 8
.

To illustrate IFC in our type system, we consider again the typing of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
from
Examples

2
and
7
.
We repeat the typing derivation and include IFC annotations, but omit processes and types to save space.
Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}
be the lattice with the only relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L\sqsubset H}}
.

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
is considered secure in our type system, and as we will show in
Sec.

5
this means that noninterference holds for this process.

However, the initial assignment of maximum secrecies to endpoints is chosen by the user.
Well typedness and thus noninterference depends on this initial choice.
To illustrate, reconsider the derivation above but now swapping the initial maximum secrecies:
