{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}
, respectively.
Since we intend to move the selections across the interface into the contexts, we need to inspect where these
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{i}}}
are bound: in the context or in the process.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}
appears in the interface (
14
), it is bound in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}}
.
We then assert that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}
actually represent the same name, and thus that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}
is bound in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}}
.
Next, we use structural congruence (
Def.

3
) to separate the selections from the rest of the processes.
The case ends with a call on the term interpretation, where the selections have been moved into the contexts.
Note that here we remove
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}
(
={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}
) from the interface, as the processes have relinquished control over this name to their respective contexts: we no longer need to monitor behavior on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}
.

Fig.

13
illustrates this case.

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}
does not appear in the interface (
15
), it is bound in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}
.
We first assert that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}
also does not appear in the interface, and thus is bound in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}
.
We then use structural congruence to identify the names to which each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{i}}}
is bound—since they are both bound, we conveniently apply alpha conversion and use
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}
in both cases—, and to separate the selections from the rest of the processes.
Finally, we call on the term interpretation, where the selections along with the binders
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu b_{i}b^{\prime})}}
are moved into the contexts.
Here, we add
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}
to the interface, as it must be used in the remainder of the processes, and thus must be monitored.

Fig.

13
shows this case.

When
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\text{{\&}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}
(
16
), the processes are expecting a selection from the contexts.
We again start with the usual well typedness check (
17
) (cf.
Def.

17
).
The purpose of our relation is to compare runs of the same process in different contexts, and so we cannot make assertions about the readiness of the contexts to make the required selection, or that these are selections of the same label.
We therefore proceed only under the condition that indeed the contexts are both ready to select the same label (
18
).
This condition uses structural congruence to identify the names in the contexts to which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is connected, conveniently referred to as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
in both contexts.
It also identifies the continuations
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
of the selections and the names
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}
to which they are connected, as well as the remainder of the contexts.
It then calls on the term evaluation (
19
), where the selections along with the restrictions binding
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
are moved into the processes.
As such,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is no longer in the interface, but now the continuations of the selections are: we add
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b}}
to the interface.

Fig.

13
illustrates this case.

Finally, we use our logical relation to define
equivalence up to observable messages
.
We say two processes are equivalent up to secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}
if they agree on their observable interface and they are related by the logical relation when placed inside any two arbitrary evaluation contexts (cf.
Def.

13
).
This ensures that, regardless of the context in which the processes run, they will behave the same with respect to the observable interface.

Definition 21

(Equivalence up to Observable Messages)
.

The relation

({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}\mathbin{@}d_{1}}}::\Gamma_{1}}})\equiv_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}\mathbin{@}d_{2}}}::\Gamma_{2}}})

holds if and only if

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{2}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}
,
and
for every
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}]}}\mathbin{@}d_{1}}}::\emptyset}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}]}}\mathbin{@}d_{2}}}::\emptyset}}
,
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}]}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}]}})\in\mathsf{E}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rrbracket
and
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}]}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}]}})\in\mathsf{E}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rrbracket
.

Example 22
.

Consider again the secure variant of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
from
Example

2
.
Anticipating noninterference, it is straightforward to check that the continuations of the initial branch are equivalent up to observable messages:

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}act\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[])}}\mathbin{@}L}}::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}:{\oplus}\{act:1,wait:1\}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[H]}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}}}:\bot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[L]}}}})

\displaystyle\equiv_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}a_{I}^{1})(a_{I}[a_{I}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}wait\mathbin{|}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}();a_{I}^{1}[])}}\mathbin{@}L}}::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}:{\oplus}\{act:1,wait:1\}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[H]}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}}}:\bot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[L]}}}})

The crucial part is that the different selections on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}}}
are unobservable.

On the other hand, consider also the insecure variant of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
from
Example

2
.
Even though their typing contexts are equal (and, hence, so are the projections onto
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}
), the continuations of the initial branch are
not
equivalent up to observable messages:

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1})(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}[a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}inf_{1}\mathbin{|}a_{I}^{1}();a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}[])}}}}::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}:{\oplus}\{inf_{1}:1,inf_{2}:1\}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[L]}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1}}}:\bot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[H]}}}})

\displaystyle\not\equiv_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1})(a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}[a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1^{\prime}}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}inf_{2}\mathbin{|}a_{I}^{1}();a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}^{1}[])}}}}::{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}:{\oplus}\{inf_{1}:1,inf_{2}:1\}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[L]}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{I}^{1}}}:\bot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[H]}}}})

Here, the different selections on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{L}}}}}

are
observable.

5
Deadlock-Sensitive Noninterference (DSNI)

Our main result is that the observable behavior (up to a given secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}
) of any well-typed process is the same when placed in different contexts.
We formalize this using our logical relation (
Def.

21
):

Theorem 23

(DSNI)
.

For all secrecy lattices
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}
, secrecy levels
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}})
and processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}}
, we have

({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}})\equiv_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}}).

Example 24
.

Following up on
Example

22
, we can conclude that DSNI holds for the secure variant of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Gov_{A}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{H}}}}}
, but not for the insecure variant.

To prove this main result, we prove a more general result (the
fundamental theorem
;
Thm.

29
) that relates two processes through
Def.

21
given that they are
observably equivalent
.
We first define precisely what we mean with observable equivalence before presenting and proving our fundamental theorem in
Sec.

5.2
.

5.1
Observable Equivalence

Towards defining observable equivalence, we want to identify the nodes (cf.
Def.

20
) of processes that can contribute to messages on observable names in the interface, referred to as
relevant nodes
.
Nodes with running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\not\sqsubseteq\xi}}
obviously cannot influence observable interface names.
However, nodes with running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\sqsubseteq\xi}}
are not necessarily capable of influencing observable interface names either.
In particular, two types of nodes with running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\sqsubseteq\xi}}
cannot influence the observable interface:

•

Nodes that input on unobservable names increase their running secrecy after the input, such that they no longer influence observable interface names.

•

Nodes that output on unobservable names can only influence nodes that input on unobservable names (and thus cannot influence observable interface names indirectly via the receiving node).

The following notion of
quasi-running secrecy
anticipates these scenarios by assigning a secrecy level to a process based on the influence of its foremost prefix corresponding to the subsequent input/output.
It is defined as the join of the current running secrecy of the process and the secrecy level of the name on which the next input/output occurs.
If either of the two levels is unobservable, the quasi-running secrecy will be unobservable.
In such cases, we know that the foremost prefix of the process cannot influence the observable interface.

Definition 25

(Quasi-running Secrecy)
.

Given a node typed
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}}
, we define the
quasi-running secrecy
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\quasi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}})}}
as follows:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\quasi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}})}}:=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqcup c}}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[]}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:1{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqcup c}}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x();P^{\prime}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:\bot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqcup c}}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqcup c}}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(z)\mathbin{\triangleright}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:\text{{\&}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqcup c}}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[a,b]}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:A\mathbin{\otimes}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}$}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqcup c}}&\text{if ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(y,z);P^{\prime}}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}}B{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}$}\end{cases}

To compute which nodes of a process are relevant, we start with nodes that have connections to the interface (through free names).
We then look at nodes that are connected to these relevant nodes through restrictions.
However, not all connections imply a possible influence on the observable interface.
Consider a node
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[a,b]}}
that is connected to a relevant node on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
: the node does not define behavior on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
but merely outputs the name, and so
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
cannot influence the observable interface through this name.
For example, in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(\nu au)(x[a,b]\mathbin{|}y(z,w);z();\ldots\mathbin{|}u[])}}
, the name
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{a}}
is not used for communication until it has been received on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
; hence, the close on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{u}}
is not considered relevant even if the send on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
were relevant.
We make this precise by defining
free communication names
: free names that are used as the subjects of unblocked prefixes.

Definition 26

(Free Communication Names)
.

We define the
free communication names
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
, denoted
\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
, as follows:

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{0}})

\displaystyle:=\emptyset

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\mathbin{|}Q}})

\displaystyle:=\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})\cup\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}})

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)P}})

\displaystyle:=\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}\}

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[]}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\}

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x();P}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\}\cup\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[a,b]}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\}

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(y,z);P}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\}\cup\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}\}

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\}

\displaystyle\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x(z)\mathbin{\triangleright}\{i:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\}\cup\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{I}}}\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{i}}})\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}\}

We now have all the ingredients to determine the relevant nodes of a process.
We define the set of relevant nodes of a process inductively by following chains of nodes connected through restriction (of which there are finitely many).
We start with nodes connected to the interface directly, and add them if their quasi-running secrecy is
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\sqsubseteq\xi}}
.
We then keep adding nodes that are connected to already relevant nodes on observable channels (names with secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\sqsubseteq\xi}}
) with quasi-running secrecy
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\sqsubseteq\xi}}
.

Definition 27

(Relevant Nodes and Binders, and Relevant Form)
.

Suppose given a process in normal form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
typed
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}}
.
Suppose every node
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\in\nodes({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
is typed
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\mathbin{@}d_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}}}}::\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}}}}
.
Given a secrecy level
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}})
, we define the set of
relevant nodes
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
, denoted
\N({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
, by induction on the size of
\binders({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
as follows (
\N({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}):=\N_{|\binders({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})|}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
):

\displaystyle\N_{0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})

\displaystyle:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\in\nodes({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})\mid\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}\in\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}).~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}})\wedge{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\quasi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\mathbin{@}d_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}}}}::\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}}}})\sqsubseteq\xi}}\}

\displaystyle\N_{n+1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})

\displaystyle:=\N_{n}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})\cup\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\in\nodes({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})~~\middle|~~\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}}\in\fcn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}).~\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}}}\in\N_{n}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}).~\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}}:A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}}}}}}.\\
\quad({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash c\sqsubseteq\xi}}\wedge\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{z}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{w}}\}\in\binders({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}))\\
{}\wedge{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\quasi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\mathbin{@}d_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}}}}::\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}}}})\sqsubseteq\xi}}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\forall 0\leq n<|\binders({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})|

We also define the set of
relevant binders
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
, denoted
\B({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
, as the subset of
\binders({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
used in the inductive step of the definition of
\N({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})
.
We then define the
relevant form
of a process in normal form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
, as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)_{\{x,y\}\in\B(P)}\mathchoice{\textstyle}{}{}{}\prod_{Q\in\N(P)}Q}}
.

Processes are then observably equivalent if their relevant nodes and relevant binders are indistinguishable (up to structural congruence).

Definition 28

(Observable Equivalence)
.

We say that two processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
are
observably equivalent
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
, if and only if there are normal forms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
respectively such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
.

5.2
The Fundamental Theorem

We now state and prove our fundamental theorem, from which DSNI (
Thm.

23
) follows.

Theorem 29

(Fundamental Theorem)
.

For all secrecy lattices
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}
, secrecy levels
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}})
and processes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}\mathbin{@}d_{1}}}::\Gamma_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}\mathbin{@}d_{2}}}::\Gamma_{2}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{2}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
, we have
({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}\mathbin{@}d_{1}}}::\Gamma_{1}}})\equiv_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}\mathbin{@}d_{2}}}::\Gamma_{2}}})
.

We first give several auxiliary results and definitions, before proving
Thm.

29
on
Sec.

5.2
:

•

Lem.

30
splits a process that reduces into an evaluation context (
Def.

13
) containing the source of the reduction originating from one of the reduction axioms in
Fig.

5
(bottom).

•

Lem.

31
splits unobservable reduction (
Def.

19
) into one of three cases: reduction internal in the context, reduction internal in the process, and communication between context and process on unobservable names.

•

Lem.

32
asserts that two observably equivalent (
Def.

28
) processes can “catch up” on each other’s unobservable reductions (
Def.

19
).
That is, if one process reduces unobservably, then the other process can do zero or one unobservable reductions such that the resulting processes are again observably equivalent.

•

Def.

33
defines a
weight
on types and typing context, which we use for induction in the proof of
Thm.

29
on
Sec.

5.2
.

Lems.

30
and
31
are proven in the on
Appendices

A
and
A
.

Lemma 30
.

Suppose given a process typed
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma}}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
, then there exists an
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E}}
for which either of the following holds:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(x[]\mathbin{|}y();Q)}}]}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}]}}
;

2.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(x[a,b]\mathbin{|}y(z,w);Q)}}]}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q\{a/z,b/w\}}}]}}
;

3.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(x[b]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}y(w)\mathbin{\triangleright}\{i:Q_{i}\}_{i\in I})}}]}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{j}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{I}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{j}\{b/w\}}}]}}
.

Lemma 31
.

Suppose
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}})\in\@netw^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
, or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}}}
, or

Lem.

30
applies, on names not in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}
.

Lemma 32

(Catch Up)
.

Suppose
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}})\in\@netw^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
, then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}\mathrel{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}^{?}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
.

Proof.

For a smoother proof, we consider a normal form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}
, and obtain from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
a normal form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
.
By
Def.

20
, the thesis follows by proving the thesis for these
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
.

By
Lem.

31
, we can distinguish three cases from which
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}}}
follows.

•

(Internal in context:
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}}}
)

The thesis holds directly with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{2}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
.

•

(Internal in process:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}}
)

By
Lem.

30
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
’s reduction is due to one of three possible synchronizations inside some evaluation context.
Note that
Lem.

30
may give us processes that are alpha variant to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}}}
; in the following we implicitly apply further alpha renaming to match the names in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
For space considerations, we sketch only the
(Close-Wait)
case; the other two cases are analogous.
Full details are in the on
Appendix

A
.

We have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{F_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)(x[]\mathbin{|}y();R)}}]}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{F_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{R}}]}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
The analysis depends on whether the close on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is a relevant node of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
or not.

If not, we derive that the wait on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
is also not relevant.
It follows by well typedness that the continuation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{R}}
will neither add relevant nodes nor influence relevancy of other nodes, so
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
and the thesis follows with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{2}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
.

If the close is indeed a relevant node of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
, we derive that the wait on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
and the binder between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
are also relevant.
By assumption, they are then also relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
, so we can derive a similar reduction to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{2}}}
.

It remains to show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{2}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
, which boils down to showing that these processes have coinciding sets of relevant nodes and binders.
Both directions of these set inclusions are analogous, so we focus on one: from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
The analysis is by induction on the construction of the sets of relevant nodes and binders.
In each case, we consider the appearance of the node: in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{R}}
or in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{F_{1}}}
.
In both cases, a thorough analysis of how the node was included as a relevant node—through a path of relevant binders and nodes that were added before—reveals an analogous relevant node in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{2}}}
.

•

(Communication between context and process on names not in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}
)

By definition, the secrecy levels of the involved names are incomparable to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}
.
Therefore, none of the nodes involved are relevant or influence relevancy of any other nodes, so
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
and the thesis holds with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q^{\prime}_{2}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
.
∎

Definition 33

(Weight)
.

The weight of a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}}
, denoted
\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}})
, is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1}})

\displaystyle:=1

\displaystyle\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\otimes}B}})

\displaystyle:=\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}})+\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{B}})+1

\displaystyle\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}})

\displaystyle:=\max_{i\in I}(\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A_{i}}}))+1

\displaystyle\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot}})

\displaystyle:=1

\displaystyle\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}}B}})

\displaystyle:=\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A}})+\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{B}})+1

\displaystyle\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\text{{\&}}\{i:A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}})

\displaystyle:=\max_{i\in I}(\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A_{i}}}))+1

The weight of a typing context
\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
is the sum of the weights of its types.

See
29

Proof.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{2}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}
.
Take any
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}]}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime}_{1}}}::\emptyset}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}]}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime}_{2}}}::\emptyset}}
.
We need to show that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}]}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}]}})\in\mathsf{E}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rrbracket
, which we do by induction on
\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
.

The first condition is that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}})\in\@netw^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
; this holds by assumption.

Next, take any
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{1}}}\mathrel{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
.
A straightforward induction on the length of these unobservable reductions shows that, by
Def.

19
and
Lem.

32
, there are
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P_{2}}}\mathrel{\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\longrightarrow}}}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longrightarrow}}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}};{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
,
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\in\@netw^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
.

Now, we need to show that, for every
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\big(\ain({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})\cup\ain({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\big)\cap\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
,

({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\in\mathsf{V}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rrbracket.

Take any such
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
.
Either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\ain({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\ain({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})
; w.l.o.g., assume the former.
The rest of the analysis depends on the type of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}
.

First, we discuss the output-like cases (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\mathbin{\otimes}}}
).
In each case, by well typedness,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is the subject of an output-like prefix in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\ain({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})
, this prefix is unguarded.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})=\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{1}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}})
, the node in which the prefix appears is relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
Therefore, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
, there is also a relevant node in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
where this prefix appears unguarded.

For space considerations, we only detail the case where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
has type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}\{i:A_{i}\}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}
; the other cases are discussed in the on
Appendix

A
.
There exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{j}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{I}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b_{1}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j}}\in\nodes({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b_{2}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j}}\in\nodes({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})
.
The analysis depends on whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
or not.

•

(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
)

By well typedness,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}\in\fn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})\cap\fn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b_{1}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x[b_{2}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
.
Similar to the case above, and since
\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}})<\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
, it follows from the IH that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}\big[x[b_{1}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}\big]}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}\big[x[b_{2}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}\big]}})\in\mathsf{E}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}}\rrbracket
.
This proves that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\in\mathsf{V}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rrbracket
.

•

(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}\notin\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
)

By well typedness,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu b_{1}b^{\prime})(x[b_{1}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}P^{\prime\prime}_{1})}}
.
The selection on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is a relevant node of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
, it is also a relevant node of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}\notin\fn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})
: otherwise,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}
, and then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{1}}}\in\fn({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu b_{2}b^{\prime})(x[b_{2}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}P^{\prime\prime}_{2})}}
.
Clearly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}::\Gamma_{1}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}::\Gamma_{2}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}\big[(\nu b_{1}b^{\prime})(x[b_{1}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}})\big]}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}}}::\emptyset}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}\big[(\nu b_{2}b^{\prime})(x[b_{2}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}})\big]}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2}}}::\emptyset}}
.
Again, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
, the chain of nodes and binders that are relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
through the binder
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu b_{1}b^{\prime})}}
has an equivalent such chain in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
through
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu b_{2}b^{\prime})}}
and the selection on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b_{2}}}
.
Hence, the effect on relevant nodes and binders by removing the binder and the selection on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is the same on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}
as it is on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
.
Clearly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{1}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}\mathbin{\Downarrow}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma_{2}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}}}
.
Also,
\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{A_{j}}})<\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\oplus}\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}}})
, so
\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}}})<\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
.
It then follows from the IH that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}\big[(\nu b_{1}b^{\prime})(x[b_{1}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}})\big]}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}\big[(\nu b_{2}b^{\prime})(x[b_{2}]\mathbin{\triangleleft}j\mathbin{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}})\big]}})\in\mathsf{E}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{b^{\prime}}}}}\rrbracket
.
This proves that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\in\mathsf{V}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rrbracket
.

Next, we discuss the negative cases (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\text{{\&}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\text{{\&}}}$}}}}}
).
In each case, by well typedness,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is the subject of an input-like prefix in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
The context
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}}
binds
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
to some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
by restriction, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}}
contains a complementary output-like prefix on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
.
Following similar reasoning, the same holds for
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\ain({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})
, this output-like prefix appears unguarded in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
To prove the thesis, we assume that this prefix also appears unguarded in
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}}
.

For space considerations, we only detail the case where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
has type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\bot{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}
; the other cases require additional care in handling continuation endpoints and are discussed in the on
Appendix

A
.
We have
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{(\nu yx)(y[]\mathbin{|}E^{\prime\prime}_{1})}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{(\nu yx)(y[]\mathbin{|}E^{\prime\prime}_{2})}}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu yx)(y[]\mathbin{|}P^{\prime}_{1})}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu yx)(y[]\mathbin{|}P^{\prime}_{2})}}
.
Clearly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}::\Gamma_{1}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}::\Gamma_{2}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime\prime}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}]}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}}}::\emptyset}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime\prime}_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}]}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2}}}::\emptyset}}
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
denote the nodes of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
, respectively, in which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
appears.
To prove that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
, it suffices to show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
and any related binders are relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
and any related binders are relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
; any connected nodes/binders follow similar reasoning.
We detail only the left-to-right direction; the other direction is analogous.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
is relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\quasi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}})\sqsubseteq\xi}}
, and thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
is also relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
through
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
in the interface.
Then also
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
is relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\quasi({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}})\sqsubseteq\xi}}
.
The analysis depends on how
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
is relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}
: (i) through the interface, or (ii) through a restriction with another relevant node.
In case (i), it follows straightforwardly that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
is also relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
In case (ii), the connected node is also relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
, and hence there is a related node that is also relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
Since the two processes agree on observable channels, the channel responsible for including
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{1}}}
as a relevant node of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}
is also bound in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
.
Then we can conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q_{2}}}
is a relevant node of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
.

Since
\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}})<\varpi({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
, it then follows from the IH that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime\prime}_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{1}}}]}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime\prime}_{2}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}]}})\in\mathsf{E}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}}\rrbracket
.
This proves that
({\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.58,0}\mathtt{E^{\prime}_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\in\mathsf{V}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}};{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rrbracket
.

Finally, we show that
\aon({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})\cap\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})=\aon({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\cap\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
.
To prove this set equality, we take any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\aon({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}})\cap\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
and prove that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\aon({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\cap\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
; the other direction is analogous.
Clearly,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
is the subject of an output-like prefix in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
, this output-like prefix must appear unguarded in a node in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
If the quasi-running secrecy of this node is observable, this node is relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\xi}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
must also have a relevant node in which the output-like prefix appears unguarded.
Otherwise, the node is not relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{1}}}
, and hence the node in which the output-like prefix appears in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
is also not relevant in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}\in\aon({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}_{2}}})\cap\dom({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{\Gamma}})
.
∎

DSNI and deadlock freedom.

As mentioned in
Sec.

3.1
, our process language is based on the finite fragment of APCP with priority mechanisms removed.
By enriching our process language with APCP’s priority mechanisms, we restrict well typedness to deadlock-free processes.
As such, our results remain relevant if we only consider deadlock-free processes.

6
Related Work

Logical relations for session types.

Existing logical relations for session types are primarily unary, focusing on proving termination
[
50
,
51
,
21
]
.
Binary logical relations have been contributed for proving parametricity
[
8
]
and noninterference
[
20
,
5
]
.
All of these logical relations are developed for intuitionistic linear session types, where process networks form trees and, as a result, neither permit cyclic networks nor deadlocks.
Whereas our logical relation has its foundations in linear session types,
it differs in that it is based on classical linear logic and allows for cycles and deadlocks.

Our work is most similar to prior work by Derakhshan et al.
[
20
]
and Balzer et al.
[
5
]
on a binary logical relation, in which the authors develop a flow-sensitive IFC type system and use the logical relation to prove noninterference.
Our IFC type system is also flow sensitive, but it is designed for an adaptation of APCP (the background of which we discuss separately) that allows for cyclic process networks and deadlocks.
Our logical relation resembles the one by the authors in that it employs an interface of names along which observations can be made. In contrast to Derakhshan et al.
[
20
]
and Balzer et al.
[
5
]
, our interface is a set of names with types, rather than a sequent that singles out the providing name from the names being used. The distinction becomes necessary in an intuitionistic linear logic setting, whereas our work is grounded in classical linear logic.
The contrast between the intuitionistic and classical setting manifests itself in other aspects of our development, too.
For example, in prior intuitionistic IFC session type systems
[
20
,
5
]
, the offering channel always caps the secrecy of the channels in the context and the running secrecy.
In our setting, however, the running secrecy of a process and the secrecy levels of its context are not necessarily related.
In particular, waiting for a channel to close may increase the running secrecy of a process.
Once the channel is closed, it disappears from the context, leaving the running secrecy entirely unrelated to the secrecies of the remaining channels.

Like our language, Derakhshan et al.
[
20
]
’s language lacks any recursion construct.
On the other hand, the possibility of deadlocks introduces a side channel similar to non-termination, which is present in the work by Balzer et al.
[
5
]
that supports general recursive session types and thus possibly looping processes.
Here, we decided to consider non-recursive processes to focus on side channels through deadlocks only and not through non-termination. In future work we would like to consider scaling our work to support general recursive session types as well. We envision employing an observation index similar to Balzer et al.
[
5
]
to stratify the logical relation in the number of observable messages exchanged over the interface. The co-presence of both non-termination and deadlocks will need careful consideration.

Our idea of an observable interface is reminiscent of the free channels with visible communications in prior work by Atkey
[
4
]
.
Atkey establishes observational equivalence for Wadler’s Classical Processes (CP) by defining a denotational semantics for CP and a logical-relations argument.
However, the logical relation in Atkey’s work does not relate two processes of a certain type but rather identifies the possible observations for each type in terms of the input/output behavior of its connectives.

Cyclic process networks.

Traditionally, (typed)
\pi
-calculi permit cyclic process networks, and as such do not guarantee deadlock freedom.
However, since the discovery of Curry-Howard correspondences between linear logic and session types
[
9
,
66
]
, the majority of works on session types restrict their network shapes to trees, such that deadlock freedom is guaranteed.
The line of work including APCP (to which our process language is highly related)
[
40
,
49
,
19
,
27
,
28
]
considers restrictions to session type systems that allow cyclic process networks without deadlocks.

IFC type systems for multiparty session types.

Capecchi et al.
[
13
,
11
]
explore secure information flow with controlled forms of declassification for multiparty sessions and prove a noninterference result via a bisimulation.
Our work differs in being flow sensitive and using a binary rather than multiparty session type paradigm.
Our use of a logical relation to show noninterference, and our foundations in linear logic also set us apart.
Follow-up works by Castellani et al.
[
14
,
12
]
also study run-time monitoring techniques to ensure secure information flow control in multiparty sessions.

IFC type systems for process calculi.

Several approaches have been explored for designing IFC type systems that prevent the leakage of information through message passing in process calculi
[
32
,
33
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
34
,
18
,
26
,
25
,
39
,
67
,
53
]
.
Some of these approaches include associating a security label with types or channels
[
34
]
, associating a security label with actions
[
33
]
, associating read and write policies with channels
[
26
,
25
]
, and associating a security label with processes and capabilities with expressions
[
15
]
.
Our approach differs from previous work in having a dynamic running secrecy that makes our system flow sensitive, using session types instead of process calculi, and the design of our novel logical relations for establishing noninterference.

Logical relations for stateful languages.

Kripke logical relations have been used to reason about stateful programs
[
52
]
.
The relation is indexed by a possible world that serves as a semantic model for the heap.
It establishes an invariant on the heap and ensures that the invariant is preserved for all future worlds.
When combined with step indexing
[
3
,
1
]
, Kripke logical relations can address circularity that arises in higher-order stores
[
2
,
22
,
23
]
.
Our logical relation is similar to Kripke logical relations in being developed for a stateful language; names, like locations, are subject to concurrent mutation.
However, our session types are rooted in linear logic and thus internalize Kripke’s logical worlds into the type system.

7
Conclusions

We have presented a new session type system with information flow control (IFC) for an asynchronous
\pi
-calculus, and a notion of noninterference by means of a logical relation between typed processes.
Our development flexibly supports realistic cyclic process networks that may deadlock.
As such, our main result is that IFC well typedness implies deadlock-sensitive noninterference (DSNI).

In future work, we plan to study the interplay between IFC / DSNI and several interesting features of message-passing concurrency, such as recursion and non-determinism.
As commented on in the previous section, we expect the notion of an observation index
[
5
]
to be applicable to circular process networks with recursive processes, although the interplay between potential leakage through non-termination and deadlocks will need careful consideration.
Support of non-determinism may be more challenging, especially when combining recursion with non-deterministic choice, which without further restriction permits loop guards at mixed confidentiality level. We will consider focusing on more curtailed but logically motivated notions of non-determinism, such as coexponentials
[
54
]
,
\mathrm{HCP}^{-}_{\mathrm{ND}}

[
41
]
, and linear non-determinism
[
63
]
.
We are also interested in exploring “name-sensitive” noninterference: the choice of names in outputs is a possible source of information leakage outside the scope of this paper.

10

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of the assumption.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
is free in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
could only have been introduced and not been altered/used thereafter.
Hence, we can inductively substitute
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}
everywhere in this derivation without affecting anything else.
The thesis follows.
∎

See
11

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
.
The base cases correspond to the seven rules in
Fig.

5
(top), and we will detail each case below.
The inductive cases correspond to closure under arbitrary process contexts in
Def.

1
; these cases follow from the IH straightforwardly.

In each base case, we need to show both directions, but the reasoning usually works in both directions trivially.
We apply inversion on the typing of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
to derive the typing of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
.

•

Rule [
sc-alpha
]:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv_{\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{\equiv}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
.
Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
are
\alpha
-equivalent, i.e., they differ in bound names only.
It is easy to see that the typing derivation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P}}
can be applied exactly to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}
by adopting the changes in bound names.

1.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.69054pt}\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}::\Delta}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 5.69054pt}\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{R}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime}_{2}}}::\Lambda}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to178.93112pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-par}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 52.6794pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Q\mathbin{|}R}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime}_{3}}}::\Delta,\Lambda}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to310.87196pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-par}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 108.23312pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P\mathbin{|}(Q\mathbin{|}R)}}\mathbin{@}d}}::\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}}\end{array}

We need to prove that the security conditions hold, in both directions.

(
\Rightarrow
)

Assume there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{1}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
.
We need to prove that there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{3}}}
such that (i)
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{3}}}
and (ii)
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{3}\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{2}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
By assumption and definition,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{2}}}
, so
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1},d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{3}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}_{3}}}
, so
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{3}}}
, proving (i).
Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{3}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{2}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
, proving (ii).

(
\Leftarrow
)

Assume there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{3}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{3}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{3}\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{2}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
We need to prove that there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{1}}}
such that (i)
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
and (ii)
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
.
By assumption and definition,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1},d^{\prime}_{3}}}
, so
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{2},d^{\prime}_{2}}}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d^{\prime}_{1}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d}}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}_{1}}}
, so
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
, proving (i).
Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{1}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime\prime}_{2}}}
, proving (ii).

Proof.

Also, by definition,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{2}\sqsupseteq d^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap d^{\prime}_{2}}}
, so, by assumption,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime}_{2}\sqsupseteq d}}
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega\Vdash d^{\prime\prime}_{2}\sqsupseteq d}}
.

\begin{array}[b]{@{}l@{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{P^{\prime}}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime}}}::\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{x}}:A{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{y}}:A^{\bot}{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{[c]}}}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}\vskip-0.99814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to113.60565pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.24812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\scriptsize{{[{typ-res}]}}}}\vskip-0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 19.394pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{\Omega}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\mathit{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{(\nu xy)P^{\prime}}}\mathbin{@}d^{\prime}}}::\Gamma}}$}\hskip 1.0pt}}}}}\end{array}
